Examples of third-party devices are: network interfaces, video acquisition devices, storage adapters.\nGPUDirect RDMA is available on both Tesla and Quadro GPUs.\nA number of limitations can apply, the most important being that the two devices must share the same upstream PCI Express root complex. Some of the limitations depend on the platform used and could be lifted in current/future products.\nA few straightforward changes must be made to device drivers to enable this functionality with a wide range of hardware devices. This document introduces the technology and describes the steps necessary to enable an GPUDirect RDMA connection to NVIDIA GPUs on Linux.\nGPUDirect RDMA within the Linux Device Driver Model\n\n1.1.\nHow GPUDirect RDMA Works\n\nWhen setting up GPUDirect RDMA communication between two peers, all physical addresses are the same from the PCI Express devices’ point of view. Within this physical address space are linear windows called PCI BARs. Each device has six BAR registers at most, so it can have up to six active 32bit BAR regions. 64bit BARs consume two BAR registers. The PCI Express device issues reads and writes to a peer device’s BAR addresses in the same way that they are issued to system memory.\nTraditionally, resources like BAR windows are mapped to user or kernel address space using the CPU’s MMU as memory mapped I/O (MMIO) addresses. However, because current operating systems don’t have sufficient mechanisms for exchanging MMIO regions between drivers, the NVIDIA kernel driver exports functions to perform the necessary address translations and mappings.\nTo add GPUDirect RDMA support to a device driver, a small amount of address mapping code within the kernel driver must be modified. This code typically resides near existing calls to\nget_user_pages()\n.\nThe APIs and control flow involved with GPUDirect RDMA are very similar to those used with standard DMA transfers.\nRefer to\nSupported Systems\nand\nPCI BAR sizes\nfor more hardware details.\n1.2.\nStandard DMA Transfer\n\nFirst, we outline a standard DMA Transfer initiated from userspace. In this scenario, the following components are present:\nUserspace program\nUserspace communication library\nKernel driver for the device interested in doing DMA transfers\nThe general sequence is as follows:\nThe userspace program requests a transfer via the userspace communication library. This operation takes a pointer to data (a virtual address) and a size in bytes.\nThe communication library must make sure the memory region corresponding to the virtual address and size is ready for the transfer. If this is not the case already, it has to be handled by the kernel driver (next step).\nThe kernel driver receives the virtual address and size from the userspace communication library. It then asks the kernel to translate the virtual address range to a list of physical pages and make sure they are ready to be transferred to or from. We will refer to this operation as pinning the memory.\nThe kernel driver uses the list of pages to program the physical device’s DMA engine(s).\nThe communication library initiates the transfer.\nAfter the transfer is done, the communication library should eventually clean up any resources used to pin the memory. We will refer to this operation as unpinning the memory.\n1.3.\nGPUDirect RDMA Transfers\n\nFor the communication to support GPUDirect RDMA transfers some changes to the sequence above have to be introduced. First of all, two new components are present:\nUserspace CUDA library\nNVIDIA kernel driver\nAs described in\nBasics of UVA CUDA Memory Management\n, programs using the CUDA library have their address space split between GPU and CPU virtual addresses, and the communication library has to implement two separate paths for them.\nThe userspace CUDA library provides a function that lets the communication library distinguish between CPU and GPU addresses. Moreover, for GPU addresses it returns additional metadata that is required to uniquely identify the GPU memory represented by the address. Refer to\nUserspace API\nfor details.\nThe difference between the paths for CPU and GPU addresses is in how the memory is pinned and unpinned. For CPU memory this is handled by built-in Linux Kernel functions (\nget_user_pages()\nand\nput_page()\n). However, in the GPU memory case the pinning and unpinning has to be handled by functions provided by the NVIDIA Kernel driver. See\nPinning GPU memory\nand\nUnpinning GPU memory\nfor details.\nSome hardware caveats are explained in\nSupported Systems\nand\nPCI BAR sizes\n.\n1.4.\nChanges in CUDA 6.0\n\nIn this section we briefly list the changes that are available in CUDA 6.0:\nCUDA peer-to-peer tokens are no longer mandatory. For memory buffers owned by the calling process (which is typical) tokens can be replaced by zero (0) in the kernel-mode function\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\n. This new feature is meant to make it easier for existing third party software stacks to adopt RDMA for GPUDirect.\nAs a consequence of the change above, a new API\ncuPointerSetAttribute()\nhas been introduced. This API must be used to register any buffer for which no peer-to-peer tokens are used. It is necessary to ensure correct synchronization behavior of the CUDA API when operation on memory which may be read by RDMA for GPUDirect. Failing to use it in these cases may cause data corruption. See changes in\nTokens Usage\n.\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\nhas been extended to return a globally unique numeric identifier, which in turn can be used by lower-level libraries to detect buffer reallocations happening in user-level code (Refer to\nUserspace API\n). It provides an alternative method to detect reallocations when intercepting CUDA allocation and deallocation APIs is not possible.\nThe kernel-mode memory pinning feature has been extended to work in combination with Multi-Process Service (MPS).\nCaveats as of CUDA 6.0:\nCUDA Unified Memory is not explicitly supported in combination with GPUDirect RDMA. While the page table returned by\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\nis valid for managed memory buffers and provides a mapping of GPU memory at any given moment in time, the GPU device copy of that memory may be incoherent with the writable copy of the page which is not on the GPU. Using the page table in this circumstance may result in accessing stale data, or data loss, because of a DMA write access to device memory that is subsequently overwritten by the Unified Memory run-time.\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\nmay be used to determine if an address is being managed by the Unified Memory runtime.\nEvery time a device memory region is pinned, new GPU BAR space is allocated unconditionally, even when pinning overlapping or duplicate device memory ranges, i.e. there is no attempt at reusing mappings. This behavior has been changed since CUDA 7.0.\n1.5.\nChanges in CUDA 7.0\n\nIn this section we briefly list the changes that are available in CUDA 7.0:\nOn the IBM POWER8 platform, GPUDirect RDMA is not supported, though it is not explicitly disabled.\nGPUDirect RDMA is not guaranteed to work on any given ARM64 platform.\nManagement of GPU BAR mappings has been improved with respect to CUDA 6.0. Now when a device memory region is pinned, GPU BAR space might be shared with pre-existing mappings. This is the case for example when pinning overlapping or duplicate device memory ranges. As a consequence, when unpinning a region, its whole BAR space will not be returned if even only a subset of its BAR space is shared.\nThe new\ncuPointerGetAttributes()\nAPI has been introduced. It can be useful when retrieving multiple attributes for the same buffer, e.g. in MPI when examining a new buffer.\ncudaPointerGetAttributes()\nis now faster since it leverages\ncuPointerGetAttributes()\ninternally.\nA new sample code,\nsamples/7_CUDALibraries/cuHook\n, has been added in CUDA 6.5. It can be used as a template for implementing an interception framework for CUDA memory de/allocation APIs.\n1.6.\nChanges in CUDA 8.0\n\nIn this section we briefly list the changes that are available in CUDA 8.0:\nThe nvidia_p2p_page_table struct has been extended to include a new member, without breaking binary compatibility. The minor version in the NVIDIA_P2P_PAGE_TABLE_VERSION macro has been updated accordingly.\nThe\nnvidia_p2p_dma_mapping\nstructure, the\nnvidia_p2p_dma_map_pages()\nand\nnvidia_p2p_dma_unmap_pages()\nAPIs, the\nNVIDIA_P2P_DMA_MAPPING_VERSION\nmacro have been introduced. These APIs can be used by third party device drivers to map and unmap the GPU BAR pages into their device’s I/O address space. The main use case is on platforms where the I/O addresses of PCIe resources, used for PCIe peer-to-peer transactions, are different from the physical addresses used by the CPU to access those same resources. See this\nlink\nfor an example of code using these new APIs.\nThe\nNVIDIA_P2P_PAGE_TABLE_VERSION_COMPATIBLE\nand\nNVIDIA_P2P_DMA_MAPPING_VERSION_COMPATIBLE\nmacros have been introduced. These are meant to be called by third-party device drivers to check for runtime binary compatibility, for example in case of changes to the data structure’s layout.\nOn the IBM POWER8 platform, when using the above APIs, GPUDirect RDMA is reported to work correctly restricted to the case where the GPU and the third party device are connected through a supported PCIe switch.\n1.7.\nChanges in CUDA 10.1\n\nGPUDirect RDMA is supported on Jetson AGX Xavier platform. Refer to the\nPorting to Tegra\nsection for details.\n1.8.\nChanges in CUDA 11.2\n\nGPUDirect RDMA is supported on Drive AGX Xavier Linux based platform. Refer to the\nPorting to Tegra\nsection for details.\n1.9.\nChanges in CUDA 11.4\n\nAdded a new a kernel module,\nnvidia-peermem\n, which provides Mellanox InfiniBand-based HCAs (Host Channel Adapters) direct peer-to-peer read and write access to the NVIDIA GPU’s video memory. Refer to\nUsing nvidia-peermem\nfor details.\nGPUDirect RDMA is supported on Jetson Orin platform. Refer to the\nPorting to Tegra\nsection for details.\nKnown Issue:\nCurrently, there is no service to automatically load\nnvidia-peermem\n. Users need to load the module manually.\n1.10.\nChanges in CUDA 12.2\n\nIn drivers released from the R515 up to the R535 branches, except for newer R525 and R535 releases mentioned below, there is a race bug which may show up as a kernel null-pointer dereference. This happens when the GPU invokes the (hereby I/O) kernel driver invalidation callback, the one which was registered during the call to\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages\n, concurrently with the I/O driver calling\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages\n.\nThe race bug does not affect the persistent mapping case, as in that case an invalidation callback is not supported nor needed.\nThe bug fix required the following API change:\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages\nno longer accepts a NULL callback pointer.\nInstead,\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages_persistent\nand\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages_persistent\nhave been introduced and should be used instead when requesting a persistent mapping.\nThe use of those new persistent APIs can be guarded by the\nNVIDIA_P2P_CAP_GET_PAGES_PERSISTENT_API\npreprocessor macro, for example when writing portable drivers.\nThe\nnvidia-peermem\nkernel module has been updated accordingly.\nAlthough deprecated when running GPU drivers from the R470 branch and newer, customers still using the off-tree\nnv_peer_mem\nmodule\n(\nhttps://github.com/Mellanox/nv_peer_memory\n) and needing the persistent mapping feature will have to switch to\nnvidia-peermem\n.\nNote that I/O drivers, which do not need persistent mappings, do not require source code changes.\nThe API changes described above are deployed in the R535 branch, specifically in release 535.14 and later, and have also been back-ported to the R525 branch, for TeslaRD3 (525.105.17) and later.\n2.\nDesign Considerations\n\nWhen designing a system to utilize GPUDirect RDMA, there a number of considerations which should be taken into account.\n2.1.\nLazy Unpinning Optimization\n\nPinning GPU device memory in BAR is an expensive operation, taking up to milliseconds. Therefore the application should be designed in a way to minimize that overhead.\nThe most straightforward implementation using GPUDirect RDMA would pin memory before each transfer and unpin it right after the transfer is complete. Unfortunately, this would perform poorly in general, as pinning and unpinning memory are expensive operations. The rest of the steps required to perform an RDMA transfer, however, can be performed quickly without entering the kernel (the DMA list can be cached and replayed using MMIO registers/command lists).\nHence, lazily unpinning memory is key to a high performance RDMA implementation. What it implies, is keeping the memory pinned even after the transfer has finished. This takes advantage of the fact that it is likely that the same memory region will be used for future DMA transfers thus lazy unpinning saves pin/unpin operations.\nAn example implementation of lazy unpinning would keep a set of pinned memory regions and only unpin some of them (for example the least recently used one) if the total size of the regions reached some threshold, or if pinning a new region failed because of BAR space exhaustion (refer to\nPCI BAR sizes\n).\n2.2.\nRegistration Cache\n\nCommunication middleware often employs an optimization called a registration cache, or pin-down cache, to minimize pinning overhead. Typically it already exists for host memory, implementing lazy unpinning, LRU de-registration, etc. For networking middleware, such caches are usually implemented in user-space, as they are used in combination with hardware capable of user-mode message injection. CUDA UVA memory address layout enables GPU memory pinning to work with these caches by taking into account just a few design considerations. In the CUDA environment, this is even more important as the amount of memory which can be pinned may be significantly more constrained than for host memory.\nAs the GPU BAR space is typically mapped using 64KB pages, it is more resource efficient to maintain a cache of regions rounded to the 64KB boundary. Even more so, as two memory areas which are in the same 64KB boundary would allocate and return the same BAR mapping.\nRegistration caches usually rely on the ability to intercept deallocation events happening in the user application, so that they can unpin the memory and free important HW resources, e.g. on the network card. To implement a similar mechanism for GPU memory, an implementation has two options:\nInstrument all CUDA allocation and deallocation APIs.\nUse a tag check function to track deallocation and reallocation. Refer to\nBuffer ID Tag Check for A Registration Cache\n.\nThere is a sample application,\n7_CUDALibraries/cuHook\n, showing how to intercept calls to CUDA APIs at run-time, which can be used to detect GPU memory de/allocations.\nWhile intercepting CUDA APIs is beyond the scope of this document, an approach to performing tag checks is available starting with CUDA 6.0. It involves the usage of the\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_BUFFER_ID\nattribute in\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\n(or\ncuPointerGetAttributes()\nif more attributes are needed) to detect memory buffer deallocations or reallocations. The API will return a different ID value in case of reallocation or an error if the buffer address is no longer valid. Refer to\nUserspace API\nfor API usage.\nNote\nUsing tag checks introduces an extra call into the CUDA API on each memory buffer use, so this approach is most appropriate when the additional latency is not a concern.\n2.3.\nUnpin Callback\n\nWhen a third party device driver pins the GPU pages with\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\nit must also provide a callback function that the NVIDIA driver will call if it needs to revoke access to the mapping.\nThis callback occurs synchronously\n, giving the third party driver the opportunity to clean up and remove any references to the pages in question (i.e., wait for outstanding DMAs to complete).\nThe user callback function may block for a few milliseconds\n, although it is recommended that the callback complete as quickly as possible. Care has to be taken not to introduce deadlocks as waiting within the callback for the GPU to do anything is not safe.\nThe callback must call\nnvidia_p2p_free_page_table()\n(not\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages()\n) to free the memory pointed to by\npage_table\n. The corresponding mapped memory areas will only be unmapped by the NVIDIA driver after returning from the callback.\nNote that the callback will be invoked in two scenarios:\nIf the userspace program explicitly deallocates the corresponding GPU memory, e.g.\ncuMemFree\n,\ncuCtxDestroy\n, etc. before the third party kernel driver has a chance to unpin the memory with\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages()\n.\nAs a consequence of an early exit of the process.\nIn the latter case there can be tear-down ordering issues between closing the file descriptor of the third party kernel driver and that of the NVIDIA kernel driver. In the case the file descriptor for the NVIDIA kernel driver is closed first, the\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages()\ncallback will be invoked.\nA proper software design is important as the NVIDIA kernel driver will protect itself from reentrancy issues with locks before invoking the callback. The third party kernel driver will almost certainly take similar actions, so dead-locking or live-locking scenarios may arise if careful consideration is not taken.\n2.4.\nSupported Systems\n\nGeneral remarks\nEven though the only theoretical requirement for GPUDirect RDMA to work between a third-party device and an NVIDIA GPU is that they share the same root complex, there exist bugs (mostly in chipsets) causing it to perform badly, or not work at all in certain setups.\nWe can distinguish between three situations, depending on what is on the path between the GPU and the third-party device:\nPCIe switches only\nsingle CPU/IOH\nCPU/IOH <-> QPI/HT <-> CPU/IOH\nThe first situation, where there are only PCIe switches on the path, is optimal and yields the best performance. The second one, where a single CPU/IOH is involved, works, but yields worse performance ( especially peer-to-peer read bandwidth has been shown to be severely limited on some processor architectures ). Finally, the third situation, where the path traverses a QPI/HT link, may be extremely performance-limited or even not work reliably.\nTip\nlspci can be used to check the PCI topology:\n$\nlspci\n-\nt\nPlatform support\nFor IBM POWER8 platform, GPUDirect RDMA and P2P are not supported, but are not explicitly disabled. They may not work at run-time.\nGPUDirect RDMA is supported on Jetson AGX Xavier platform starting from CUDA 10.1 and on Drive AGX Xavier Linux based platforms from CUDA 11.2. Refer to\nPorting to Tegra\nfor details. On ARM64, the necessary peer-to-peer functionality depends on both the hardware and the software of the particular platform. So while GPUDirect RDMA is not explicitly disabled on non-Jetson and non-Drive platforms, there are no guarantees that it will be fully functional.\nIOMMUs\nGPUDirect RDMA currently relies upon all physical addresses being the same from the different PCI devices’ point of view. This makes it incompatible with IOMMUs performing any form of translation other than 1:1, hence they must be disabled or configured for pass-through translation for GPUDirect RDMA to work.\n2.5.\nPCI BAR sizes\n\nPCI devices can ask the OS/BIOS to map a region of physical address space to them. These regions are commonly called BARs. NVIDIA GPUs currently expose multiple BARs, and some of them can back arbitrary device memory, making GPUDirect RDMA possible.\nThe maximum BAR size available for GPUDirect RDMA differs from GPU to GPU. For example, currently the smallest available BAR size on Kepler class GPUs is 256 MB. Of that, 32MB are currently reserved for internal use. These sizes may change.\nOn some Tesla-class GPUs a large BAR feature is enabled, e.g. BAR1 size is set to 16GB or larger. Large BARs can pose a problem for the BIOS, especially on older motherbords, related to compatibility support for 32bit operating systems. On those motherboards the bootstrap can stop during the early POST phase, or the GPU may be misconfigured and so unusable. If this appears to be occuring it might be necessary to enable some special BIOS feature to deal with the large BAR issue. Please consult your system vendor for more details regarding large BAR support.\n2.6.\nTokens Usage\n\nWarning\nStarting in CUDA 6.0, tokens should be considered deprecated, though they are still supported.\nAs can be seen in\nUserspace API\nand\nKernel API\n, one method for pinning and unpinning memory requires two tokens in addition to the GPU virtual address.\nThese tokens,\np2pToken\nand\nvaSpaceToken\n, are necessary to uniquely identify a GPU VA space. A process identifier alone does not identify a GPU VA space.\nThe tokens are consistent within a single CUDA context (i.e., all memory obtained through\ncudaMalloc()\nwithin the same CUDA context will have the same\np2pToken\nand\nvaSpaceToken\n). However, a given GPU virtual address need not map to the same context/GPU for its entire lifetime. As a concrete example:\ncudaSetDevice\n(\n0\n)\nptr0\n=\ncudaMalloc\n();\ncuPointerGetAttribute\n(\n&\nreturn_data\n,\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\n,\nptr0\n);\n// Returns [p2pToken = 0xabcd, vaSpaceToken = 0x1]\ncudaFree\n(\nptr0\n);\ncudaSetDevice\n(\n1\n);\nptr1\n=\ncudaMalloc\n();\nassert\n(\nptr0\n==\nptr1\n);\n// The CUDA driver is free (although not guaranteed) to reuse the VA,\n// even on a different GPU\ncuPointerGetAttribute\n(\n&\nreturn_data\n,\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\n,\nptr0\n);\n// Returns [p2pToken = 0x0123, vaSpaceToken = 0x2]\nThat is, the same address, when passed to\ncuPointerGetAttribute\n, may return different tokens at different times during the program’s execution. Therefore, the third party communication library must call\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\nfor every pointer it operates on.\nSecurity implications\nThe two tokens act as an authentication mechanism for the NVIDIA kernel driver. If you know the tokens, you can map the address space corresponding to them, and the NVIDIA kernel driver doesn’t perform any additional checks. The 64bit\np2pToken\nis randomized to prevent it from being guessed by an adversary.\nWhen no tokens are used, the NVIDIA driver limits the\nKernel API\nto the process which owns the memory allocation.\n2.7.\nSynchronization and Memory Ordering\n\nGPUDirect RDMA introduces a new independent GPU data flow path exposed to third party devices and it is important to understand how these devices interact with the GPU’s relaxed memory model.\nProperly registering a BAR mapping of CUDA memory is required for that mapping to remain consistent with CUDA APIs operations on that memory.\nOnly CUDA synchronization and work submission APIs provide memory ordering of GPUDirect RDMA operations.\nRegistration for CUDA API Consistency\nRegistration is necessary to ensure the CUDA API memory operations visible to a BAR mapping happen before the API call returns control to the calling CPU thread. This provides a consistent view of memory to a device using GPUDirect RDMA mappings when invoked after a CUDA API in the thread. This is a strictly more conservative mode of operation for the CUDA API and disables optimizations, thus it may negatively impact performance.\nThis behavior is enabled on a per-allocation granularity either by calling\ncuPointerSetAttribute()\nwith the\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_SYNC_MEMOPS\nattribute, or p2p tokens are retrieved for a buffer when using the legacy path. Refer to\nUserspace API\nfor more details.\nAn example situation would be Read-after-Write dependency between a\ncuMemcpyDtoD()\nand subsequent GPUDirect RDMA read operation on the destination of the copy. As an optimization the device-to-device memory copy typically returns asynchronously to the calling thread after queuing the copy to the GPU scheduler. However, in this circumstance that will lead to inconsistent data read via the BAR mapping, so this optimization is disabled an the copy completed before the CUDA API returns.\nCUDA APIs for Memory Ordering\nOnly CPU initiated CUDA APIs provide ordering of GPUDirect memory operations as observed by the GPU. That is, despite a third party device having issued all PCIE transactions, a running GPU kernel or copy operation may observe stale data or data that arrives out-of-order until a subsequent CPU initiated CUDA work submission or synchronization API. To ensure that memory updates are visible to CUDA kernels or copies, an implementation should ensure that all writes to the GPU BAR happen before control is returned to the CPU thread which will invoke the dependent CUDA API.\nAn example situation for a network communication scenario is when a network RDMA write operation is completed by the third party network device and the data is written to the GPU BAR mapping. Though reading back the written data either through GPU BAR or a CUDA memory copy operation, will return the newly written data, a concurrently running GPU kernel to that network write might observe stale data, the data partially written, or the data written out-of-order.\nIn short, a GPU kernel is wholly inconsistent with concurrent RDMA for GPUDirect operations and accessing the memory overwritten by the third party device in such a situation would be considered a data race. To resolve this inconsistency and remove the data race the DMA write operation must complete with respect to the CPU thread which will launch the dependent GPU kernel.\n3.\nHow to Perform Specific Tasks\n\n3.1.\nDisplaying GPU BAR space\n\nStarting in CUDA 6.0 the NVIDIA SMI utility provides the capability to dump BAR1 memory usage. It can be used to understand the application usage of BAR space, the primary resource consumed by GPUDirect RDMA mappings.\n$ nvidia-smi -q\n...\n      BAR1 Memory Usage\n         Total                       : 256 MiB\n         Used                        : 2 MiB\n         Free                        : 254 MiB\n...\nGPU memory is pinned in fixed size chunks, so the amount of space reflected here might be unexpected. In addition, a certain amount of BAR space is reserved by the driver for internal use, so not all available memory may be usable via GPUDirect RDMA. Note that the same ability is offered programmatically through the\nnvmlDeviceGetBAR1MemoryInfo()\nNVML API.\n3.2.\nPinning GPU memory\n\nCorrect behavior requires using\ncuPointerSetAttribute()\non the memory address to enable proper synchronization behavior in the CUDA driver. Refer to\nSynchronization and Memory Ordering\n.\nvoid\npin_buffer\n(\nvoid\n*\naddress\n,\nsize_t\nsize\n)\n{\nunsigned\nint\nflag\n=\n1\n;\nCUresult\nstatus\n=\ncuPointerSetAttribute\n(\n&\nflag\n,\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_SYNC_MEMOPS\n,\naddress\n);\nif\n(\nCUDA_SUCCESS\n==\nstatus\n)\n{\n// GPU path\npass_to_kernel_driver\n(\naddress\n,\nsize\n);\n}\nelse\n{\n// CPU path\n// ...\n}\n}\nThis is required so that the GPU memory buffer is treated in a special way by the CUDA driver, so that CUDA memory transfers are guaranteed to always be synchronous with respect to the host. Refer to\nUserspace API\nfor details on\ncuPointerSetAttribute()\n.\nIn the kernel driver, invoke\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\n.\n// for boundary alignment requirement\n#define GPU_BOUND_SHIFT   16\n#define GPU_BOUND_SIZE    ((u64)1 << GPU_BOUND_SHIFT)\n#define GPU_BOUND_OFFSET  (GPU_BOUND_SIZE-1)\n#define GPU_BOUND_MASK    (~GPU_BOUND_OFFSET)\nstruct\nkmd_state\n{\nnvidia_p2p_page_table_t\n*\npage_table\n;\n// ...\n};\nvoid\nkmd_pin_memory\n(\nstruct\nkmd_state\n*\nmy_state\n,\nvoid\n*\naddress\n,\nsize_t\nsize\n)\n{\n// do proper alignment, as required by NVIDIA kernel driver\nu64\nvirt_start\n=\naddress\n&\nGPU_BOUND_MASK\n;\nsize_t\npin_size\n=\n(\naddress\n+\nsize\n-\nvirt_start\n+\nGPU_BOUND_SIZE\n-\n1\n)\n&\nGPU_BOUND_MASK\n;\nif\n(\n!\nsize\n)\nreturn\n-\nEINVAL\n;\nint\nret\n=\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages\n(\n0\n,\n0\n,\nvirt_start\n,\npin_size\n,\n&\nmy_state\n->\npage_table\n,\nfree_callback\n,\n&\nmy_state\n);\nif\n(\nret\n==\n0\n)\n{\n// Succesfully pinned, page_table can be accessed\n}\nelse\n{\n// Pinning failed\n}\n}\nNote how the start address is aligned to a 64KB boundary before calling the pinning functions.\nIf the function succeeds the memory has been pinned and the\npage_table\nentries can be used to program the device’s DMA engine. Refer to\nKernel API\nfor details on\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\n.\n3.3.\nUnpinning GPU memory\n\nIn the kernel driver, invoke\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages()\n.\nvoid\nunpin_memory\n(\nvoid\n*\naddress\n,\nsize_t\nsize\n,\nnvidia_p2p_page_table_t\n*\npage_table\n)\n{\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages\n(\n0\n,\n0\n,\naddress\n,\nsize\n,\npage_table\n);\n}\nRefer to\nKernel API\nfor details on\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages()\n.\nStarting CUDA 6.0 zeros should be used as the token parameters. Note that\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages()\nmust be called from within the same process context as the one from which the corresponding\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\nhas been issued.\n3.4.\nHandling the free callback\n\nThe NVIDIA kernel driver invokes\nfree_callback(data)\nas specified in the\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\ncall if it needs to revoke the mapping. Refer to\nKernel API\nand\nUnpin Callback\nfor details.\nThe callback waits for pending transfers and then cleans up the page table allocation.\nvoid\nfree_callback\n(\nvoid\n*\ndata\n)\n{\nmy_state\n*\nstate\n=\ndata\n;\nwait_for_pending_transfers\n(\nstate\n);\nnvidia_p2p_free_pages\n(\nstate\n->\npage_table\n);\n}\nThe NVIDIA kernel driver handles the unmapping so\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages()\nshould not be called.\n3.5.\nBuffer ID Tag Check for A Registration Cache\n\nRemember that a solution built around Buffer ID tag checking is not recommended for latency sensitive implementations. Instead, instrumentation of CUDA allocation and deallocation APIs to provide callbacks to the registration cache is recommended, removing tag checking overhead from the critical path.\nThe first time a device memory buffer is encountered and recognized as not yet pinned, the pinned mapping is created and the associated buffer ID is retrieved and stored together in the cache entry. The\ncuMemGetAddressRange()\nfunction can be used to obtain the size and starting address for the whole allocation, which can then be used to pin it. As\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\nwill need a pointer aligned to 64K, it is useful to directly align the cached address. Also, as the BAR space is currently mapped in chunks of 64KB, it is more resource efficient to round the whole pinning to 64KB.\n// struct buf represents an entry of the registration cache\nstruct\nbuf\n{\nCUdeviceptr\npointer\n;\nsize_t\nsize\n;\nCUdeviceptr\naligned_pointer\n;\nsize_t\naligned_size\n;\nint\nis_pinned\n;\nuint64_t\nid\n;\n// buffer id obtained right after pinning\n};\nOnce created, every time a registration cache entry will be used it must be first checked for validity. One way to do this is to use the Buffer ID provided by CUDA as a tag to check for deallocation or reallocation.\nint\nbuf_is_gpu_pinning_valid\n(\nstruct\nbuf\n*\nbuf\n)\n{\nuint64_t\nbuffer_id\n;\nint\nretcode\n;\nassert\n(\nbuf\n->\nis_pinned\n);\n// get the current buffer id\nretcode\n=\ncuPointerGetAttribute\n(\n&\nbuffer_id\n,\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_BUFFER_ID\n,\nbuf\n->\npointer\n);\nif\n(\nCUDA_ERROR_INVALID_VALUE\n==\nretcode\n)\n{\n// the device pointer is no longer valid\n// it could have been deallocated\nreturn\nERROR_INVALIDATED\n;\n}\nelse\nif\n(\nCUDA_SUCCESS\n!=\nretcode\n)\n{\n// handle more serious errors here\nreturn\nERROR_SERIOUS\n;\n}\nif\n(\nbuf\n->\nid\n!=\nbuffer_id\n)\n// the original buffer has been deallocated and the cached mapping should be invalidated and the buffer re-pinned\nreturn\nERROR_INVALIDATED\n;\nreturn\n0\n;\n}\nWhen the buffer identifier changes the corresponding memory buffer has been reallocated so the corresponding kernel-space page table will not be valid anymore. In this case the kernel-space\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\ncallback would have been invoked. Thus the Buffer IDs provide a tag to keep the pin-down cache consistent with the kernel-space page table without requiring the kernel driver to up-call into the user-space.\nIf\nCUDA_ERROR_INVALID_VALUE\nis returned from\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\n, the program should assume that the memory buffer has been deallocated or is otherwise not a valid GPU memory buffer.\nIn both cases, the corresponding cache entry must be invalidated.\n// in the registration cache code\nif\n(\nbuf\n->\nis_pinned\n&&\n!\nbuf_is_gpu_pinning_valid\n(\nbuf\n))\n{\nregcache_invalidate_entry\n(\nbuf\n);\npin_buffer\n(\nbuf\n);\n}\n3.6.\nLinking a Kernel Module against nvidia.ko\n\nRun the extraction script:\n./NVIDIA-Linux-x86_64-<version>.run -x\nThis extracts the NVIDA driver and kernel wrapper.\nNavigate to the output directory:\ncd <output directory>/kernel/\nWithin this directory, build the NVIDIA module for your kernel:\nmake module\nAfter this is done, the\nModule.symvers\nfile under your kernel build directory contains symbol information for\nnvidia.ko\n.\nModify your kernel module build process with the following line:\nKBUILD_EXTRA_SYMBOLS := <path to kernel build directory>/Module.symvers\n3.7.\nUsing nvidia-peermem\n\nThe NVIDIA GPU driver package provides a kernel module,\nnvidia-peermem\n, which provides NVIDIA InfiniBand based HCAs (Host Channel Adapters) direct peer-to-peer read and write access to the NVIDIA GPU’s video memory. It allows GPUDirect RDMA-based applications to use GPU computing power with the RDMA interconnect without needing to copy data to host memory.\nThis capability is supported with NVIDIA ConnectX®-3 VPI or newer adapters. It works with both InfiniBand and RoCE (RDMA over Converged Ethernet) technologies.\nNVIDIA OFED (Open Fabrics Enterprise Distribution), or MLNX_OFED, introduces an API between the InfiniBand Core and peer memory clients such as NVIDIA GPUs. The\nnvidia-peermem\nmodule registers the NVIDIA GPU with the InfiniBand subsystem by using peer-to-peer APIs provided by the NVIDIA GPU driver.\nThe kernel must have the required support for RDMA peer memory either through additional patches to the kernel or via MLNX_OFED as a prerequisite for loading and using\nnvidia-peermem\n.\nIt is possible that the\nnv_peer_mem\nmodule from the GitHub project may be installed and loaded on the system. Installation of\nnvidia-peermem\nwill not affect the functionality of the existing\nnv_peer_mem\nmodule. But, to load and use\nnvidia-peermem\n, users must disable the\nnv_peer_mem\nservice. Additionally, it is encouraged to uninstall the\nnv_peer_mem\npackage to avoid any conflict with\nnvidia-peermem\nsince only one module can be loaded at any time.\nTo stop the\nnv_peer_mem\nservice:\n# service nv_peer_mem stop</screen>\nCheck if\nnv_peer_mem.ko\nis still loaded after stopping the service:\n# lsmod | grep nv_peer_mem\nIf\nnv_peer_mem.ko\nis still loaded, unload it using:\n# rmmod nv_peer_mem\nUninstall the\nnv_peer_mem\npackage:\nFor DEB-based OS:\n# dpkg -P nvidia-peer-memory\n# dpkg -P nvidia-peer-memory-dkms\nFor RPM-based OS:\n# rpm -e nvidia_peer_memory\nAfter ensuring kernel support and installing the GPU driver,\nnvidia-peermem\ncan be loaded with the following command with root privileges in a terminal window:\n# modprobe nvidia-peermem\nNote\nNote: If the NVIDIA GPU driver is installed before MLNX_OFED, the GPU driver must be uninstalled and installed again to make sure\nnvidia-peermem\nis compiled with the RDMA APIs that are provided by MLNX_OFED.\n4.\nReferences\n\n4.1.\nBasics of UVA CUDA Memory Management\n\nUnified virtual addressing (UVA) is a memory address management system enabled by default in CUDA 4.0 and later releases on Fermi and Kepler GPUs running 64-bit processes. The design of UVA memory management provides a basis for the operation of GPUDirect RDMA. On UVA-supported configurations, when the CUDA runtime initializes, the virtual address (VA) range of the application is partitioned into two areas: the CUDA-managed VA range and the OS-managed VA range. All CUDA-managed pointers are within this VA range, and the range will always fall within the first 40 bits of the process’s VA space.\nCUDA VA Space Addressing\n\nSubsequently, within the CUDA VA space, addresses can be subdivided into three types:\nGPU\nA page backed by GPU memory. This will not be accessible from the host and the VA in question will never have a physical backing on the host. Dereferencing a pointer to a GPU VA from the CPU will trigger a segfault.\nCPU\nA page backed by CPU memory. This will be accessible from both the host and the GPU at the same VA.\nFREE\nThese VAs are reserved by CUDA for future allocations.\nThis partitioning allows the CUDA runtime to determine the physical location of a memory object by its pointer value within the reserved CUDA VA space.\nAddresses are subdivided into these categories at page granularity; all memory within a page is of the same type. Note that GPU pages may not be the same size as CPU pages. The CPU pages are usually 4KB and the GPU pages on Kepler-class GPUs are 64KB. GPUDirect RDMA operates exclusively on GPU pages (created by\ncudaMalloc()\n) that are within this CUDA VA space.\n4.2.\nUserspace API\n\nData structures\ntypedef\nstruct\nCUDA_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS_st\n{\nunsigned\nlong\nlong\np2pToken\n;\nunsigned\nint\nvaSpaceToken\n;\n}\nCUDA_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\n;\nFunction cuPointerSetAttribute()\nCUresult\ncuPointerSetAttribute\n(\nvoid\n*\ndata\n,\nCUpointer_attribute\nattribute\n,\nCUdeviceptr\npointer\n);\nIn GPUDirect RDMA scope, the interesting usage is when\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_SYNC_MEMOPS\nis passed as the\nattribute\n:\nunsigned\nint\nflag\n=\n1\n;\ncuPointerSetAttribute\n(\n&\nflag\n,\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_SYNC_MEMOPS\n,\npointer\n);\nParameters\ndata [in]\nA pointer to a\nunsigned\nint\nvariable containing a boolean value.\nattribute [in]\nIn GPUDirect RDMA scope should always be\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_SYNC_MEMOPS\n.\npointer [in]\nA pointer.\nReturns\nCUDA_SUCCESS\nif pointer points to GPU memory and the CUDA driver was able to set the new behavior for the whole device memory allocation.\nanything else\nif pointer points to CPU memory.\nIt is used to explicitly enable a strictly synchronizing behavior on the whole memory allocation pointed to by\npointer\n, and by doing so disabling all data transfer optimizations which might create problems with concurrent RDMA and CUDA memory copy operations. This API has CUDA synchronizing behavior, so it should be considered expensive and possibly invoked only once per buffer.\nFunction cuPointerGetAttribute()\nCUresult\ncuPointerGetAttribute\n(\nconst\nvoid\n*\ndata\n,\nCUpointer_attribute\nattribute\n,\nCUdeviceptr\npointer\n);\nThis function has two different attributes related to GPUDirect RDMA:\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\nand\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_BUFFER_ID\n.\nWarning\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS has been deprecated in CUDA 6.0\nWhen\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\nis passed as the\nattribute\n,\ndata\nis a pointer to\nCUDA_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\n:\nCUDA_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\ntokens\n;\ncuPointerGetAttribute\n(\n&\ntokens\n,\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\n,\npointer\n);\nIn this case, the function returns two tokens for use with the\nKernel API\n.\nParameters\ndata [out]\nStruct\nCUDA_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\nwith the two tokens.\nattribute [in]\nIn GPUDirect RDMA scope should always be\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_P2P_TOKENS\n.\npointer [in]\nA pointer.\nReturns\nCUDA_SUCCESS\nif pointer points to GPU memory.\nanything else\nif pointer points to CPU memory.\nThis function may be called at any time, including before CUDA initialization, and it has CUDA synchronizing behavior, as in\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_SYNC_MEMOPS\n, so it should be considered expensive and should be invoked only once per buffer.\nNote that values set in\ntokens\ncan be different for the same\npointer\nvalue during a lifetime of a user-space program. Refer to\nTokens Usage\nfor a concrete example.\nNote that for security reasons the value set in\np2pToken\nwill be randomized, to prevent it from being guessed by an adversary.\nIn CUDA 6.0, a new attribute has been introduced that is useful to detect memory reallocations.\nWhen\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_BUFFER_ID\nis passed as the\nattribute\n,\ndata\nis expected to point to a 64bit unsigned integer variable, like\nuint64_t\n.\nuint64_t\nbuf_id\n;\ncuPointerGetAttribute\n(\n&\nbuf_id\n,\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_BUFFER_ID\n,\npointer\n);\nParameters\ndata [out]\nA pointer to a 64 bits variable where the buffer id will be stored.\nattribute [in]\nThe\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_BUFFER_ID\nenumerator.\npointer [in]\nA pointer to GPU memory.\nReturns\nCUDA_SUCCESS\nif pointer points to GPU memory.\nanything else\nif pointer points to CPU memory.\nSome general remarks follow:\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\nand\ncuPointerSetAttribute()\nare CUDA driver API functions only.\nIn particular,\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\nis not equivalent to\ncudaPointerGetAttributes()\n, as the required functionality is only present in the former function. This in no way limits the scope where GPUDirect RDMA may be used as\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\nis compatible with the CUDA Runtime API.\nNo runtime API equivalent to\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\nis provided. This is so as the additional overhead associated with the CUDA runtime API to driver API call sequence would introduce unneeded overhead and\ncuPointerGetAttribute()\ncan be on the critical path, e.g. of communication libraries.\nWhenever possible, we suggest to combine multiple calls to\ncuPointerGetAttribute\nby using\ncuPointerGetAttributes\n.\nFunction ``cuPointerGetAttributes()``\nCUresult\ncuPointerGetAttributes\n(\nunsigned\nint\nnumAttributes\n,\nCUpointer_attribute\n*\nattributes\n,\nvoid\n**\ndata\n,\nCUdeviceptr\nptr\n);\nThis function can be used to inspect multiple attributes at once. The one most probably related to GPUDirect RDMA are\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_BUFFER_ID\n,\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_MEMORY_TYPE\nand\nCU_POINTER_ATTRIBUTE_IS_MANAGED\n.\n4.3.\nKernel API\n\nThe following declarations can be found in the\nnv-p2p.h\nheader that is distributed in the NVIDIA Driver package. Please refer to the inline documentation contained in that header file for a detailed description of the parameters and the return values of the functions described below.\nPreprocessor macros\nNVIDIA_P2P_PAGE_TABLE_VERSION_COMPATIBLE()\nand\nNVIDIA_P2P_DMA_MAPPING_VERSION_COMPATIBLE()\npreprocessor macros are meant to be called by third-party device drivers to check for runtime binary compatibility.\nStructure nvidia_p2p_page\ntypedef\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_page\n{\nuint64_t\nphysical_address\n;\nunion\nnvidia_p2p_request_registers\n{\nstruct\n{\nuint32_t\nwreqmb_h\n;\nuint32_t\nrreqmb_h\n;\nuint32_t\nrreqmb_0\n;\nuint32_t\nreserved\n[\n3\n];\n}\nfermi\n;\n}\nregisters\n;\n}\nnvidia_p2p_page_t\n;\nIn the\nnvidia_p2p_page\nstructure only the\nphysical_address\nfield is relevant to GPUDirect RDMA.\nStructure nvidia_p2p_page_table\ntypedef\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_page_table\n{\nuint32_t\nversion\n;\nuint32_t\npage_size\n;\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_page\n**\npages\n;\nuint32_t\nentries\n;\nuint8_t\n*\ngpu_uuid\n;\n}\nnvidia_p2p_page_table_t\n;\nThe\nversion\nfield of the page table should be checked by using\nNVIDIA_P2P_PAGE_TABLE_VERSION_COMPATIBLE()\nbefore accessing the other fields.\nThe\npage_size\nfield is encoded according to the\nnvidia_p2p_page_size_type\nenum.\nStructure nvidia_p2p_dma_mapping\ntypedef\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_dma_mapping\n{\nuint32_t\nversion\n;\nenum\nnvidia_p2p_page_size_type\npage_size_type\n;\nuint32_t\nentries\n;\nuint64_t\n*\ndma_addresses\n;\n}\nnvidia_p2p_dma_mapping_t\n;\nThe version field of the dma mapping should be passed to\nNVIDIA_P2P_DMA_MAPPING_VERSION_COMPATIBLE()\nbefore accessing the other fields.\nFunction nvidia_p2p_get_pages()\nint\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages\n(\nuint64_t\np2p_token\n,\nuint32_t\nva_space_token\n,\nuint64_t\nvirtual_address\n,\nuint64_t\nlength\n,\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_page_table\n**\npage_table\n,\nvoid\n(\n*\nfree_callback\n)(\nvoid\n*\ndata\n),\nvoid\n*\ndata\n);\nThis function makes the pages underlying a range of GPU virtual memory accessible to a third-party device.\nWarning\nThis is an expensive operation and should be performed as infrequently as possible - refer to\nLazy Unpinning Optimization\n.\nFunction nvidia_p2p_put_pages()\nint\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages\n(\nuint64_t\np2p_token\n,\nuint32_t\nva_space_token\n,\nuint64_t\nvirtual_address\n,\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_page_table\n*\npage_table\n);\nThis function releases a set of pages previously made accessible to a third-party device. Warning: it is not meant to be called from within the\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\ncallback.\nFunction nvidia_p2p_free_page_table()\nint\nnvidia_p2p_free_page_table\n(\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_page_table\n*\npage_table\n);\nThis function frees a third-party P2P page table and is meant to be invoked during the execution of the\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\ncallback.\nFunction nvidia_p2p_dma_map_pages()\nint\nnvidia_p2p_dma_map_pages\n(\nstruct\npci_dev\n*\npeer\n,\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_page_table\n*\npage_table\n,\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_dma_mapping\n**\ndma_mapping\n);\nThis function makes the physical pages retrieved using\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\naccessible to a third-party device.\nIt is required on platforms where the I/O addresses of PCIe resources, used for PCIe peer-to-peer transactions, are different from the physical addresses used by the CPU to access those same resources.\nOn some platforms, this function relies on a correct implementation of the\ndma_map_resource()\nLinux kernel function.\nFunction nvidia_p2p_dma_unmap_pages()\nint\nnvidia_p2p_dma_unmap_pages\n(\nstruct\npci_dev\n*\npeer\n,\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_page_table\n*\npage_table\n,\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_dma_mapping\n*\ndma_mapping\n);\nThis function unmaps the physical pages previously mapped to the third-party device by\nnvidia_p2p_dma_map_pages()\n.\nIt is not meant to be called from within the\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\ninvalidation callback.\nFunction nvidia_p2p_free_dma_mapping()\nint\nnvidia_p2p_free_dma_mapping\n(\nstruct\nnvidia_p2p_dma_mapping\n*\ndma_mapping\n);\nThis function is meant to be called from within the\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\ninvalidation callback.\nNote that the deallocation of the I/O mappings may be deferred, for example after returning from the invalidation callback.\n4.4.\nPorting to Tegra\n\nGPUDirect RDMA is supported on Jetson AGX Xavier platform from CUDA 10.1, on DRIVE AGX Xavier Linux based platforms from CUDA 11.2 and on Jetson Orin platform from CUDA 11.4. From this point onwards, this document will collectively refer Jetson and Drive as Tegra. Owing to hardware and software specific divergence of Tegra vis-a-vis Linux-Desktop, already developed applications needs to be slightly modified in order to port them to Tegra. The following sub-sections (4.4.1-4.4.3) briefs over the necessary changes.\n4.4.1.\nChanging the allocator\n\nGPUDirect RDMA on Desktop allows applications to operate exclusively on GPU pages allocated using\ncudaMalloc()\n. On Tegra, applications will have to change the memory allocator from\ncudaMalloc()\nto\ncudaHostAlloc()\n. Applications can either:\nTreat the returned pointer as if it is a device pointer, provided that the iGPU supports UVA or\ncudaDevAttrCanUseHostPointerForRegisteredMem\ndevice attribute is a non-zero value when queried using\ncudaDeviceGetAttribute()\nfor iGPU.\nGet the device pointer corresponding to the host memory allocated using\ncudaHostGetDevicePointer()\n. Once the application has the device pointer, all the rules that are applicable to the standard GPUDirect solution also apply to Tegra.\n4.4.2.\nModification to Kernel API\n\nThe declarations under Tegra API column of the following table can be found in the nv-p2p.h header that is distributed in the NVIDIA Driver package. Refer to the inline documentation contained in that header file for a detailed description of the parameters and the return values. The table below represents the Kernel API changes on Tegra vis-a-vis Desktop.\nDesktop API\nTegra API\nint nvidia_p2p_get_pages(uint64_t p2p_token, uint32_t va_space_token, uint64_t virtual_address, uint64_t length, struct nvidia_p2p_page_table **page_table, void ( *free_callback)(void *data), void *data);\nint nvidia_p2p_get_pages(u64 virtual_address, u64 length, struct nvidia_p2p_page_table **page_table, void (*free_callback)(void *data), void *data);\nint nvidia_p2p_put_pages(uint64_t p2p_token, uint32_t va_space_token, uint64_t virtual_address, struct nvidia_p2p_page_table *page_table);\nint nvidia_p2p_put_pages(struct nvidia_p2p_page_table *page_table);\nint nvidia_p2p_dma_map_pages(struct pci_dev *peer, struct nvidia_p2p_page_table *page_table, struct nvidia_p2p_dma_mapping **dma_mapping);\nint nvidia_p2p_dma_map_pages(struct device *dev, struct nvidia_p2p_page_table *page_table, struct nvidia_p2p_dma_mapping **dma_mapping, enum dma_data_direction direction);\nint nvidia_p2p_dma_unmap_pages(struct pci_dev *peer, struct nvidia_p2p_page_table *page_table, struct nvidia_p2p_dma_mapping *dma_mapping);\nint nvidia_p2p_dma_unmap_pages(struct nvidia_p2p_dma_mapping *dma_mapping);\nint nvidia_p2p_free_page_table(struct nvidia_p2p_page_table *page_table);\nint nvidia_p2p_free_page_table(struct nvidia_p2p_page_table *page_table);\nint nvidia_p2p_free_dma_mapping(struct nvidia_p2p_dma_mapping *dma_mapping);\nint nvidia_p2p_free_dma_mapping(struct nvidia_p2p_dma_mapping *dma_mapping);\n4.4.3.\nOther highlights\n\nThe length of the requested mapping and base address must be a multiple of 4KB, failing which leads to an error.\nUnlike the Desktop version, callback registered at\nnvidia_p2p_get_pages()\nwill always be triggered when\nnvidia_p2p_put_pages()\nis invoked. It is the reponsibilty of the kernel driver to free the page_table allocated by calling\nnvidia_p2p_free_page_table()\n. Note that, similar to the Desktop version, the callback will also triggered in scenarios explained in\nUnpin Callback\n.\nSince\ncudaHostAlloc()\ncan be allocated with\ncudaHostAllocWriteCombined\nflag or default flag, applications are expected to excercise caution when mapping the memory to userspace, for example using standard linux\nmmap()\n. In this regard:\nWhen GPU memory is allocated as writecombined, the userspace mapping should also be done as writecombined by passing the\nvm_page_prot\nmember of\nvm_area_struct\nto the standard linux interface:\n`pgprot_writecombine()\n<\nhttps://elixir.bootlin.com/linux/latest/source/arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h#L403\n>`__.\nWhen GPU memory is allocated as default, no modifcations to the\nvm_page_prot\nmember of\nvm_area_struct\nshould be done.\nIncompatible combination of map and allocation attributes will lead to undefined behavior.\n5.\nNotices\n\n5.1.\nNotice\n\nThis document is provided for information purposes only and shall not be regarded as a warranty of a certain functionality, condition, or quality of a product.
